\title[Anomalous scaling limit]{Anomalous scaling limit of a Brownian particle in a log-correlated potential}

\author[S. Armstrong]{Scott Armstrong}

\date{}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
We study a Brownian particle in~$\Rd$, $d\geq2$, with drift given by the gradient of a log-correlated Gaussian potential. At weak disorder, we prove convergence to a scaling limit whose law is singular with respect to Brownian motion and whose invariant measure is given by Gaussian multiplicative chaos. The proof uses a renormalization group induction: at each scale, the elliptic operator is well approximated by a Laplacian with effective diffusivity decaying as a power of scale. We compute this exponent exactly in dimension two.
\end{abstract}

\maketitle

\tableofcontents

\section{Introduction}

\subsection{The diffusion and its scaling limit}

We study a Brownian particle in~$\Rd$, $d\geq2$, with drift given by the gradient of a log-correlated Gaussian potential~$\log\a$. At weak disorder, we prove that the particle is subdiffusive and converges, after rescaling, to a diffusion whose law is singular with respect to Brownian motion.

The position~$X_t$ solves the stochastic differential equation
\begin{equation}
\label{e.sde.intro}
	dX_t
	=
	\nabla\log\a(X_t)\,dt+\sqrt2\,dW_t,
	\qquad
	X_0=x\in\Rd\,,
\end{equation}
where~$W$ is a standard Brownian motion on~$\Rd$.

The potential~$\log\a$ is built from independent random fields~$\g_0,\g_1,\g_2,\ldots$ at scales~$1,3,3^2,\ldots$. The field~$\g_0$ is a smooth, stationary, centered Gaussian field with finite range of dependence: its restrictions to sufficiently distant sets are independent. Its size is measured by the \textbf{disorder strength}~$\gmcdelta\in(0,\nf12]$. The field~$\g_k$ is a copy of~$\g_0$ stretched by the factor~$3^k$: it has the law of~$\g_0(3^{-k}\,\cdot)$. We set
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.anchored.coefficient}
	\a(x)
	\coloneqq
	\exp\left(
	\sum_{k=0}^\infty(\g_k(x)-\g_k(0))
	\right).
\end{equation}
The subtraction of the values at the origin makes the series converge locally in~$C^{1,1}$. Each field whose scale is much smaller than~$|x-y|$ takes independent values at~$x$ and~$y$, the number of such fields grows logarithmically with~$|x-y|$, and the fields whose scales are much larger than~$|x-y|$ change little between~$x$ and~$y$. Consequently, for all~$x,y\in\Rd$ with~$|x-y|\geq2$, the variance of~$\log\a(x)-\log\a(y)$ lies between two positive multiples of~$\log|x-y|$ that depend only on~$d$ and the law of~$\g_0$. An environment~$\omega$ is a realization of the random fields, and we write~$\P$ and~$\E$ for the law and expectation of the environment. We call the law of the trajectories in a fixed environment \textbf{quenched} and its average over the environment \textbf{annealed}.

\smallskip

For a bounded stationary potential with sufficiently rapid decay of correlations, homogenization theory shows that, on large scales, the particle behaves like a Brownian motion with a deterministic positive diffusivity, called the effective diffusivity. Here, independent fluctuations of equal strength occur at each of the scales~$1,3,3^2,\ldots$. If the sum defining~$\log\a$ is cut off at a large scale, the particle in the resulting potential has an effective diffusivity, and the cumulative effect of these fluctuations drives it to zero as the cutoff scale grows. We prove that it decays at least as a power of that scale, making the particle \textbf{subdiffusive}: the time needed to travel a large distance grows faster than the square of that distance.

\smallskip

If~$\gmcdelta$ is smaller than a threshold that depends only on~$d$, Theorem~\ref{t.A} constructs a unique limiting diffusion~$Z$ and proves convergence to it. Let~$\ahom_N$ denote the effective diffusivity of the coefficient with infrared cutoff at scale~$3^N$, which keeps only the fields~$\g_0,\ldots,\g_N$ and is normalized to have mean one (see~\eqref{e.intro.cutoff.coefficients} and the paragraph after it), and let~$\tau^2\coloneqq\log\E[\exp(\g_0(0))]$. For a particle started at the origin, the convergence and the leading weak-disorder estimate for~$\log\ahom_N$ take the informal form
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.roughly}
	\bigl(3^{-N}X_{3^{2N}t/\ahom_N}\bigr)_{t\geq0}
	\longrightarrow
	(Z_t)_{t\geq0}
	\qquad\text{with}\qquad
	\ahom_N\approx e^{-\nf{2\tau^2(N+1)}{d}}
	\quad\text{as } N\to\infty\,,
\end{equation}
where the displayed approximation to~$\ahom_N$ comes from an estimate of its logarithm, with error bounded in~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic}. The limit~$Z$ is a continuous, conservative, strong Markov diffusion. Its paths are rougher than Brownian paths, its law is singular with respect to Brownian motion, and its invariant measure is obtained from Gaussian multiplicative chaos: the limit, as the smoothing scale tends to zero, of the random measures whose density is the exponential of a smoothed log-correlated field, divided by its mean. In dimension two, the estimate of~$\ahom_N$ is an identity,~$\ahom_N=e^{-(N+1)\tau^2}$, so time is rescaled by exactly~$3^{2N}e^{(N+1)\tau^2}$.

\smallskip

The precise assumptions, which also allow non-Gaussian fields, are~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4} below. Fix~$d\geq2$ and the disorder strength~$\gmcdelta\in(0,\nf12]$, which bounds the size of the fields in~\ref{a.g2}. On a unit cube, the suprema of~$|\g_0|$,~$|\nabla\g_0|$ and~$|\nabla^2\g_0|$ are at most of order~$\gmcdelta$, with Gaussian tails. Let~$\g_0,\g_1,\g_2,\ldots$ be independent random fields such that, for every~$k\in\N_0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.same.law.shift}
	\g_k
	\quad\text{and}\quad
	\g_0(3^{-k}\,\cdot)
	\quad\text{have the same law}\,.
\end{equation}
For~$m\in\Z$, let~$\cu_m\coloneqq(-\nf12\,3^m,\nf12\,3^m)^d$ be the cube of side~$3^m$; in particular,~$\cu_0=(-\nf12,\nf12)^d$. We use the maximum norm on~$\Rd$ and the dual norm on gradients; in~\ref{a.g2}, the norm of the Hessian is the Lipschitz constant of~$\nabla\g_0$ on~$\cu_0$ with respect to these norms. We assume the following.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\textbf{g\arabic*})]
\setcounter{enumi}{0}

\item
\label{a.g1}
The field~$\g_0$ is stationary, has mean zero, and has range of dependence~$\sqrt d$.

\item
\label{a.g2}
Almost surely,~$\g_0\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rd)$, and
\begin{equation}
\label{e.coefficient.field.regularity}
	\E\left[\exp\left(\gmcdelta^{-2}\left(\|\g_0\|_{L^\infty(\cu_0)}+\|\nabla\g_0\|_{L^\infty(\cu_0)}+\|\nabla^2\g_0\|_{L^\infty(\cu_0)}\right)^2\right)\right]\leq2\,.
\end{equation}
\item
\label{a.g3}
The law of~$\g_0$ is invariant under signed permutations of the coordinates and under~$\g_0\mapsto-\g_0$.

\item
\label{a.g4}
The number~$\tau^2=\log\E[\exp(\g_0(0))]$ is positive.
\end{enumerate}
By~\ref{a.g2} and the symmetry~$\g_0\mapsto-\g_0$ in~\ref{a.g3}, there exists~$C(d)<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{e.tau.bound}
	\tau^2\leq C\gmcdelta^2
	\quad\text{and}\quad
	\left|
	\tau^2-\frac12\E[|\g_0(0)|^2]
	\right|
	\leq C\gmcdelta^4\,.
\end{equation}
A statement holds \textbf{at weak disorder} if it holds whenever~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, for a threshold~$\gmcdelta_0>0$ depending only on~$d$ and the indicated parameters.

Throughout,~$X$ denotes the continuous Feller diffusion with generator~$\a^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\a\nabla)$ that we construct in Section~\ref{s.fixed.coefficient} from its resolvent; there we also show that, from every starting point, its law is that of the solution of~\eqref{e.sde.intro}. It is reversible with respect to~$\a(x)\,dx$.

For~$L\in\N_0$, the coefficient with \textbf{infrared cutoff} at scale~$3^L$ retains only the fields with scales at most~$3^L$, and we call~$3^L$ the \textbf{cutoff scale}:
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.cutoff.coefficients}
	\a_L(x)
	\coloneqq
	\exp\left(
	\sum_{k=0}^L(\g_k(x)-\tau^2)
	\right).
\end{equation}
The coefficient~$\a_L$ is stationary, has range of dependence of order~$3^L$, and has mean one. Subtracting~$\tau^2$, rather than the values at the origin as in~\eqref{e.intro.anchored.coefficient}, normalizes the mean and leaves the drift unchanged. Let~$X^{(L)}$ be the diffusion constructed in the same way with~$\a_L$ in place of~$\a$; its generator is
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.cutoff.generator}
	\a_L^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\a_L\nabla)
	=
	\Delta+\nabla\log\a_L\cdot\nabla\,.
\end{equation}
The homogenized matrix of~$\a_L$ is the constant matrix such that, in domains much larger than~$3^L$, solutions of~$-\nabla\cdot(\a_L\nabla u)=f$ are close to solutions of the same equation with this constant matrix in place of~$\a_L$. By the symmetry~\ref{a.g3}, it is a scalar multiple of the identity. We denote this scalar by~$\ahom_L$ and call it the \textbf{effective diffusivity}. It measures how fast the particle~$X^{(L)}$ moves on the cutoff scale: at weak disorder, when started at the center of a cube of side~$3^L$, it takes time of order~$3^{2L}/\ahom_L$ to leave the cube, on average over the environment and the trajectories. The same holds for the particle~$X$ without cutoff (Lemma~\ref{lim:lem-mean-exit}). This is why~\eqref{e.intro.roughly} rescales time by~$3^{2N}/\ahom_N$ when it rescales space by~$3^{-N}$.

Theorem~\ref{t.renormalized.diffusivities} shows that~$\ahom_m$ is nonincreasing in~$m$ and that, for every~$m\in\N_0$, it is at least~$e^{-(m+1)\tau^2}$ and at most a negative power of~$3^{m+1}$ whose exponent depends only on~$d$ and the law of~$\g_0$. We also estimate the logarithm of~$\ahom_m$: there exist~$\gmcdelta_0(d)>0$ and~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, if~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then, for every~$m\in\N_0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic}
	\left|
	\log\ahom_m+\frac{2\tau^2(m+1)}d
	\right|
	\leq
	C\gmcdelta^2|\log\gmcdelta|(1+m\gmcdelta)\,.
\end{equation}
In dimension two, planar duality gives the exact identity~$\ahom_m=e^{-(m+1)\tau^2}$ for every~$m\in\N_0$: rotation by a right angle exchanges gradient fields and divergence-free fields, which makes the homogenized matrices of~$\a_m$ and~$\a_m^{-1}$ inverse to each other, and~$\a_m^{-1}$ has the law of~$e^{2(m+1)\tau^2}\a_m$ by the symmetry~$\g_0\mapsto-\g_0$. When~$m\gmcdelta\geq1$ and~$\tau^2$ is at least a fixed positive multiple of~$\gmcdelta^2$, the error in~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic} is smaller than the leading term by a factor of order~$\gmcdelta|\log\gmcdelta|$.

\smallskip

\begin{theoremA}[Scaling limit]
\label{t.A}
Assume~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}. There exists~$\gmcdelta_0(d)>0$ such that, if~$0<\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then there is a continuous strong Markov process~$Z$ on~$\Rd$ in a random environment, with quenched laws~$\mathbf P_x$ and expectations~$\mathbf E_x$ when started at~$x$, such that the following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \underline{\emph{Convergence}}: For every~$x\in\Rd$, as the integer~$N$ tends to infinity, the annealed law of the process~$(3^{-N}X_{3^{2N}t/\ahom_N})_{t\geq0}$ with~$X_0=3^Nx$ converges to the annealed law of~$Z$ started at~$x$.
\item \underline{\emph{Reversible measure}}: Almost surely,~$Z$ is reversible with respect to a measure~$\mu$ that is singular with respect to Lebesgue measure.
\item \underline{\emph{Anomalous scaling}}: There exist~$C<\infty$ and~$\eta>0$, depending only on~$d$ and the law of~$\g_0$, such that, if~$\sigma_r$ denotes the first time at which~$Z$ leaves the cube of side~$r$ centered at its starting point, then, for every~$x\in\Rd$ and~$r\in(0,1]$,
\begin{equation*}
 \E\bigl[\mathbf E_x[\sigma_r]\bigr]\leq Cr^{2+\eta}\,.
\end{equation*}
For every~$x\in\Rd$, almost surely,~$\mathbf P_x$-almost every path satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \limsup_{t\downarrow0}\,t^{-\gamma}|Z_t-Z_0|=\infty
 \qquad\text{for every }\gamma>\frac1{2+\eta}\,.
\end{equation*}
In dimension two, one can take~$\eta=\tau^2/\log3$.
\item \underline{\emph{Non-Gaussian marginals}}: Almost surely, for every~$x\in\Rd$ and~$t>0$, the law of~$Z_t$ under~$\mathbf P_x$ is absolutely continuous with respect to~$\mu$ and is not Gaussian.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theoremA}

Theorem~\ref{t.A} is a simplified form of Theorem~\ref{t.scaling.limit}. There, all the rescaled processes are realized in a single environment, and their quenched laws converge almost surely. Since rescaling space by~$3^{-N}$ turns the field with scale~$3^k$ into a field with scale~$3^{k-N}$, this environment consists of independent fields at all scales~$3^j$ with~$j\in\Z$. The measure~$\mu$ is the almost-sure limit of the reversible measures of the rescaled processes, suitably normalized. Apart from a continuous positive factor that comes from the fields with scales larger than one, it is stationary; for Gaussian fields, this stationary part is a Gaussian multiplicative chaos measure.

Brownian motion needs a time of order~$r^2$ to leave a cube of side~$r$. The slow motion of~$X$ over large distances corresponds to fast motion of~$Z$ over small distances: by~(iii), the expected time that~$Z$ needs to leave a small cube is much smaller than the square of its side. These rapid exits make the paths of~$Z$ rougher than Brownian paths: their pointwise H\"older exponent at time zero is at most~$1/(2+\eta)$, below the Brownian value~$\nf12$. In particular, by the law of the iterated logarithm,~$\mathbf P_x$ is singular with respect to every mixture of laws of Brownian motions. If~$\g_0$ is Gaussian, then~$\tau^2=\nf12\operatorname{Var}[\g_0(0)]$, so in dimension two this exponent is at most~$\bigl(2+\operatorname{Var}[\g_0(0)]/(2\log3)\bigr)^{-1}$.

\subsection{Quantitative homogenization}

We compare solutions of the elliptic equation for the cutoff coefficient divided by its effective diffusivity with solutions of the same problem for the Laplacian. The error remains controlled as the cutoff grows, although the ratio of the supremum to the infimum of the coefficient on the domain, which controls the constants in classical elliptic estimates, grows with the cutoff. For~$0\leq L\leq M$, define
\begin{equation*}
	A_{L,M}(x)
	\coloneqq
	\ahom_L^{-1}\a_L(3^Mx),
	\qquad x\in \cu_0\,.
\end{equation*}
The coefficient~$A_{L,M}$ is stationary, its homogenized matrix is the identity, and its range of dependence is of order~$3^{L-M}$. For~$f\in L^2(\cu_0)$ and~$h\in H^2(\cu_0)$, let~$u_{L,M},u_{\mathrm{hom}}\in H^1(\cu_0)$ solve the problems with the same source and boundary values:
\begin{equation*}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
	-\nabla\cdot(A_{L,M}\nabla u_{L,M})&=f
	&&\text{in }\cu_0,\\
	u_{L,M}&=h
	&&\text{on }\partial \cu_0,
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\qquad
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
	-\Delta u_{\mathrm{hom}}&=f
	&&\text{in }\cu_0,\\
	u_{\mathrm{hom}}&=h
	&&\text{on }\partial \cu_0,
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
\end{equation*}
and measure the homogenization error by
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal D_{L,M}
	\coloneqq
	\|u_{L,M}-u_{\mathrm{hom}}\|_{L^2(\cu_0)}
	+
	\|\nabla u_{L,M}-\nabla u_{\mathrm{hom}}\|_{H^{-1}(\cu_0)}
	+
	\|A_{L,M}\nabla u_{L,M}-\nabla u_{\mathrm{hom}}\|_{H^{-1}(\cu_0)}\,.
\end{equation*}
Both~$u_{L,M}$ and~$\mathcal D_{L,M}$ depend on the data~$f$ and~$h$, which we suppress from the notation. For smooth data,~$u_{L,M}$ has a probabilistic interpretation in terms of the diffusion with generator~$\nabla\cdot(A_{L,M}\nabla)$, which is a time change of the particle~$X^{(L)}$ with space rescaled by~$3^{-M}$. With~$f=1$ and~$h=0$,~$u_{L,M}$ is the expected exit time from~$\cu_0$; with~$f=0$, it is the expected value of~$h$ at the exit position.

\begin{theoremA}[Quantitative homogenization of the Dirichlet problem]
\label{t.B}
Assume~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}. The following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \underline{\emph{Estimate uniform in the cutoff}}: For every~$\vartheta\in(0,1)$ and~$q\in[1,\infty)$, there exist~$\gmcdelta_0(\vartheta,q,d)>0$ and~$C(\vartheta,q,d)<\infty$ such that, if~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then there exist random variables~$\mathcal Z_{L,M}\geq1$ satisfying
\begin{equation*}
	\sup_{0\leq L\leq M}
	\|\mathcal Z_{L,M}\|_{L^q(\P)}
	\leq C
\end{equation*}
such that, almost surely, for every~$L$,~$M$,~$f$ and~$h$,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal D_{L,M}
	\leq
	\mathcal Z_{L,M}\,\gmcdelta^\vartheta
	\left(\|f\|_{L^2(\cu_0)}+\|h\|_{H^2(\cu_0)}\right).
\end{equation*}
If~$f=0$, then the factor~$\gmcdelta^\vartheta$ may be replaced by~$C\gmcdelta$.

\item \underline{\emph{Algebraic rate at a fixed cutoff}}: There exists~$\alpha_{\mathrm{hom}}(d)>0$ such that, for every~$L\in\N_0$ and~$q\in[1,\infty)$, there exists a random variable~$\mathcal Y_{L,q}\geq1$ with~$\|\mathcal Y_{L,q}\|_{L^q(\P)}\leq C(L,q,d,\gmcdelta)$ such that, almost surely, for every~$M\geq L$ and every~$f$ and~$h$,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal D_{L,M}
	\leq
	\mathcal Y_{L,q}\,3^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{hom}}(M-L)}
	\left(\|f\|_{L^2(\cu_0)}+\|h\|_{H^2(\cu_0)}\right).
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theoremA}

In~(i), the error is small because the disorder is weak, not because the domain is large: the estimate covers the case~$M=L$, in which the range of dependence is comparable to the size of the domain, and it is uniform in~$L$. Part~(ii) does not assume weak disorder: at a fixed cutoff, enlarging the domain gives algebraic decay of the error, with a prefactor that may depend on~$L$ and~$\gmcdelta$. Section~\ref{s.Holder.regularity} proves the estimate in~(i) in Theorem~\ref{t.cutoff.Dirichlet.homogenization} and the fixed-cutoff rate in Theorem~\ref{t.fixed.cutoff.Dirichlet.algebraic}.

In probabilistic terms, Theorem~\ref{t.B} compares these expected exit times and exit distributions with their Brownian counterparts in~$L^2(\cu_0)$. The generator of the particle in~\eqref{e.sde.intro} also contains the factor~$\a^{-1}$: the process is determined by its Dirichlet form, the energy~$\int\a|\nabla u|^2\,dx$, together with its reversible measure~$\a\,dx$. Its scaling limit therefore requires the convergence of both the energies and the reversible measures of the rescaled processes. Theorem~\ref{t.B} does not give the convergence of the energies, since its bound in~(i) does not tend to zero as the cutoff grows, and the proof of Theorem~\ref{t.A} does not use it (see Figure~\ref{f.dependence}).

\subsection{Large-scale regularity and a Liouville theorem}

To show that the energies of the rescaled diffusions have subsequential limits and that all these limits coincide, we need to control the oscillation of~$\a_L$-harmonic functions, that is, of solutions of~$\nabla\cdot(\a_L\nabla u)=0$, and the energy that they carry on small cubes. We obtain this control uniformly in~$L$, with H\"older exponents approaching one as~$\gmcdelta\to0$. For a fixed exponent, the estimates compare a cube on which the function is~$\a_L$-harmonic with much smaller subcubes; the number of scales that must separate the two is random, with a tail bound uniform in the cutoff. For a measurable set~$U$ of finite positive volume and~$p\in[1,\infty)$, write~$(u)_U\coloneqq\fint_Uu$ and~$\|u\|_{\underline L^p(U)}\coloneqq(\fint_U|u|^p)^{\nf1p}$.

\begin{theoremA}[Large-scale H\"older regularity and a Liouville theorem]
\label{t.C}
Assume~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}. There exist~$\gmcdelta_0(d)>0$,~$C_0(d)<\infty$ and~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, if~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then the following statements hold with
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.regularity.exponent}
	\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}
	\coloneqq
	1-C_0\gmcdelta|\log\gmcdelta|^{\nf12}\,.
\end{equation}
In both statements,~$L$ ranges over~$\N_0\cup\{\infty\}$, and~$\a_L$ is read as~$\a$ when~$L=\infty$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \underline{\emph{Large-scale H\"older estimate}}: Let~$\gamma\in[\nf12,\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}]$. For every~$L$ and~$m\in\N$, there exists an~$\N$-valued random variable~$\mathcal L_L(\gamma,m)$ such that, for every~$k\in\N$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.regularity.minimal.scale}
	\P[\mathcal L_L(\gamma,m)>k]
	\leq
	C\exp\left(
	-\frac{(1-\gamma)^2(k-C)_+}
	{C\gmcdelta^2|\log\gmcdelta|}
	\right).
\end{equation}
Moreover, almost surely, for every~$L$ and~$m$, if~$u\in H^1(\cu_m)$ is~$\a_L$-harmonic in~$\cu_m$, then, for every~$n\in\N_0$ satisfying~$n\leq m-\mathcal L_L(\gamma,m)$ and every~$z\in3^n\Zd$ with~$z+\cu_n\subseteq\cu_{m-1}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.large.scale.Holder}
	\left\|
	u-(u)_{z+\cu_n}
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(z+\cu_n)}
	\leq
	C3^{-\gamma(m-n)}
	\left\|
	u-(u)_{\cu_m}
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(\cu_m)}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{e.intro.large.scale.energy}
	\left\|\a_L^{\nf12}\nabla u
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(z+\cu_n)}
	\leq
	C3^{(1-\gamma)(m-n)}
	\left\|\a_L^{\nf12}\nabla u
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(\cu_m)}\,.
\end{equation}

\item \underline{\emph{Liouville theorem}}: Almost surely, for every~$L$, if~$u\in H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rd)$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
	-\nabla\cdot(\a_L\nabla u)=0
	\quad\text{in }\Rd
	\quad\text{and}\quad
	\liminf_{R\to\infty}
	R^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}}
	\inf_{c\in\mathbb R}
	\|u-c\|_{\underline L^2(B_R)}=0\,,
\end{equation*}
then~$u$ is constant.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theoremA}

The energy estimate~\eqref{e.intro.large.scale.energy} prevents a fixed proportion of the energy from concentrating on a small number of the small cubes. More precisely, if the energy in~$\cu_m$ is positive, then every family of cubes~$z+\cu_n$, with~$n$ and~$z$ as in~\eqref{e.intro.large.scale.energy}, that carries a fixed positive proportion of that energy contains at least a positive multiple of~$3^{(d-2(1-\gamma))(m-n)}$ cubes, where the multiple depends on~$d$ and on that proportion. Taking~$\gamma=\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}$ makes the exponent in this count approach~$d$ as~$\gmcdelta\to0$.

Theorem~\ref{t.C} is proved as Theorem~\ref{t.large.scale.Holder.multifractal} in Section~\ref{s.Holder.regularity}.

\subsection{Comparison with previous work}
\label{ss.lit.review}

Exponentials of log-correlated Gaussian fields are the basic objects of Gaussian multiplicative chaos, introduced by~\citet{Kahane}; see~\citet*{RVsurvey} and~\citet{Berestycki1} for modern accounts and~\citet*{DRSV} for an overview of log-correlated fields. Random walks and diffusions in random force fields, and the anomalous diffusion they produce, were studied in the physics literature by~\citet{Fisher},~\citet*{KLY},~\citet*{FFQSS} and~\citet*{BCGLD}; see~\citet*{BG} for a review. For drifts given by gradients of log-correlated Gaussian potentials, renormalization group computations predicted a subdiffusive power law with an exponent depending on the disorder strength; see~\citet*[Section~4.3.2.3]{BG}.

For Gaussian fields, in the normalization of~\eqref{e.sde.intro}, the variance of~$\log\a(x)-\log\a(y)$ is asymptotic to~$4\tau^2\log|x-y|/\log3$ as~$|x-y|\to\infty$, and the weak-disorder formula of~\citet*[equation~(3)]{CastilloLeDoussal} predicts that the particle needs a time of order~$r^{2+2\tau^2/(d\log3)}$ to travel a distance~$r$. The estimate~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic} gives this leading exponent for the time scale~$3^{2N}/\ahom_N$ in~\eqref{e.intro.roughly}, and in dimension two the identity~$\ahom_N=e^{-(N+1)\tau^2}$ gives exactly the predicted exponent~$2+\tau^2/\log3$.

\smallskip

\citet*{BDG} studied the planar lattice analogue of~\eqref{e.sde.intro}, a random walk among conductances given by exponentials of the discrete Gaussian free field, and proved recurrence, return-probability estimates, and exponents for expected exit times. When the field is multiplied by a small constant,~\citet*{DW} proved tightness of the random quenched path laws for a version of this walk whose conductances are determined by a smooth approximation to the field. In both models, the walk chooses a neighbor with probability proportional to the conductance of the edge joining them, and the reversible measure gives each vertex the sum of the conductances of its incident edges. The field therefore affects both the transition probabilities and the reversible measure, as in the continuum diffusion~\eqref{e.sde.intro}.

For the walk studied by~\citet{BDG},~\citet*[Problem~16.13]{BiskupSurvey} formulated the scaling-limit problem through a continuum diffusion whose drift is the gradient of a smooth approximation to the planar Gaussian free field, and asked for joint convergence of the field and the diffusion, after a suitable change of time, to a field and a process with continuous, nonconstant paths. Theorem~\ref{t.A} proves existence and uniqueness of a scaling limit for the continuum diffusion~\eqref{e.sde.intro} with log-correlated potentials satisfying~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}, at weak disorder and in every dimension~$d\geq2$.

\smallskip

Liouville Brownian motion, constructed by~\citet*{GRV} and by~\citet{Berestycki2}, is a time change of planar Brownian motion using the Gaussian multiplicative chaos measure of the planar Gaussian free field. Its Dirichlet form is the unweighted energy~$\int|\nabla u|^2\,dx$ on the~$L^2$ space of that measure. For a measure with a smooth positive density~$\a$, this construction gives the generator~$\a^{-1}\Delta$, so the field changes only the time parametrization of the Brownian trajectories. The diffusion~\eqref{e.sde.intro} instead has energy~$\int\a|\nabla u|^2\,dx$ on~$L^2(\a\,dx)$ and generator~$\a^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\a\nabla)$, so the field also enters the energy. Identifying the limit of these weighted energies is the main step of this paper.

\smallskip

\citet*{ASD} proved power-law superdiffusion for a diffusion in a self-similar incompressible random drift with sufficiently small positive Hurst exponent, using an induction across scales for its elliptic operator. We use the same deterministic coarse-graining framework, in which the constants in elliptic estimates on a cube are given not by pointwise bounds on the coefficient but by two coarse-grained matrices, effective coefficients of the cube defined by variational problems, and we normalize these estimates by the effective diffusivity at each scale. The incompressible drift increases the effective diffusivity; the gradient drift reduces it. In addition to estimates across scales, the present paper constructs a scaling limit.

\subsection{Overview of the proofs}

The proof has three parts. The first is a renormalization group analysis of the divergence-form elliptic operators with the cutoff coefficients: by induction across scales, we show that, on cubes whose side is the cutoff scale, the operator is close to the effective diffusivity times the Laplacian, uniformly in the cutoff, although the pointwise ellipticity of the coefficient deteriorates as the cutoff grows. The second part, the central step of the paper, shows that the energies of the rescaled diffusions have a unique limit. This limit is a random Dirichlet form for which there is no formula, so we cannot identify it by computing it. Instead, we compare two subsequential limits through the optimal constants in a two-sided bound between them: unless the two forms are proportional, replacing a solution for one form by harmonic functions for the other on small cubes would improve these constants. The third part pairs the limit form with the limit of the reversible measures and proves convergence of the processes. In the list below, steps~(1)--(3) form the first part, step~(4) the second and step~(5) the third, and step~(6) derives the properties of the limit.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Coarse-graining (Section~\ref{s.coarse.graining}).} We recall elliptic estimates whose constants are given by coarse-grained matrices instead of pointwise bounds on the coefficient.
\item \textbf{Induction across scales (Sections~\ref{s.infrared.cutoffs}--\ref{s.homogenized.diffusivities}).} On a cube whose side is the cutoff scale, the coarse-grained matrices of the cutoff coefficient are close to the effective diffusivity times the identity, uniformly in the cutoff. Perturbative estimates for the effective diffusivities give~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic}, and a separate argument proves their power-law decay without assuming weak disorder.
\item \textbf{Large-scale regularity and Dirichlet homogenization (Section~\ref{s.Holder.regularity}).} Approximation of solutions by harmonic functions on the scales where it holds, together with a bound on the proportion of scales where it fails, gives Theorem~\ref{t.C} and bounds the energy that a solution carries in small cubes. Theorem~\ref{t.B}, also proved there, follows from the estimates of steps~(1) and~(2) and does not need the regularity estimates.
\item \textbf{Uniqueness of the limit form (Section~\ref{sec:mfd-uniqueness}).} Two subsequential limits of the energies bound each other by deterministic constants. The optimal constants in this comparison coincide, so the limits are proportional, and homogenization on large cubes shows that the proportionality factor is one.
\item \textbf{Convergence of the process (Section~\ref{sec:mfd-process-convergence}).} The unique limit form, the limiting reversible measure and tightness give convergence of the path laws in probability. Comparing the energies at two finite cutoffs gives estimates with summable error probabilities, and the Borel--Cantelli lemma upgrades this to almost-sure convergence.
\item \textbf{Properties of the limit (Section~\ref{lim:sec-process-measure}).} Power-law decay of the effective diffusivities gives the exit-time bounds and the singularity of the path laws with respect to Brownian motion. The transition laws are absolutely continuous with respect to~$\mu$, which is singular with respect to Lebesgue measure and charges no hyperplane, so no transition law is Gaussian.
\end{enumerate}
Figure~\ref{f.dependence} records how the main results depend on each other. The appendices contain moment bounds, concentration inequalities and comparisons of Besov norms.

\subsubsection{Coarse-grained matrices}

The ratio of the supremum to the infimum of the cutoff coefficient on a cube whose side is the cutoff scale grows with the cutoff, so elliptic estimates based on pointwise bounds give no control uniform in the cutoff. We use instead the two \textbf{coarse-grained matrices} of a cube, following the deterministic theory of~\citet*{AK.HC} and~\citet{ASD} recalled in Section~\ref{s.coarse.graining}. The first gives the minimal energy of a function with affine boundary values of a prescribed slope. The inverse of the second gives the energy of the solution of the Neumann problem whose boundary flux is that of a prescribed constant vector field. For a constant coefficient, both matrices equal the coefficient. For a variable coefficient, their difference controls the error in approximating solutions by solutions of a constant-coefficient equation. The coarse-grained matrices of subcubes control the constants in Poincar\'e and Caccioppoli inequalities and in harmonic approximation. Bounding them relative to the effective diffusivity on cubes whose side is the cutoff scale gives estimates that remain useful as the effective diffusivity tends to zero.

\subsubsection{Induction across scales and the effective diffusivities}

Proposition~\ref{p.coarse.grained.bound} shows that, for each fixed~$p<\infty$ and for~$\gmcdelta$ small enough depending on~$p$, both coarse-grained matrices of~$\a_m$ on~$\cu_m$ are within a relative distance of order~$\gmcdelta$ of~$\ahom_m\Id$ in~$L^p(\P)$, uniformly in~$m$. We prove it by induction on the scale. Suppose that the estimate holds for~$\a_L$ on cubes of side~$3^L$, and let~$m>L$. On~$\cu_m$, the coefficient~$\a_L$ still has range of dependence of order~$3^L$, so its restrictions to well-separated subcubes are independent, and averaging over these subcubes contracts the error, the gap between the two coarse-grained matrices. The contraction comes from the variational structure of these matrices, as in~\citet*{AKMBook}. The error on a cube is bounded by two terms. The first consists of the spatial averages of the gradient of an optimizer over subcubes, measured in a negative Sobolev norm; it is small because the coefficient is independent on well-separated subcubes. The second is the decrease of the error from the subcubes to the cube. Since the expected error does not increase with the scale, these decreases add up to a bounded quantity, so the second term is small on most scales. The estimates of~\citet{AK.HC} bound both terms with constants given by coarse-grained matrices. We then add the fields~$\g_{L+1},\ldots,\g_m$. They vary on scales up to~$3^m$ and need not be nearly constant on~$\cu_m$, but at weak disorder the ratio~$\a_m/\a_L$, normalized by~$\ahom_m/\ahom_L$, is close to one in the moments that control the coarse-grained matrices. On a subcube, the fields whose scales are much larger than the subcube are nearly constant, and multiplying a coefficient by a constant multiplies both coarse-grained matrices by that constant. We choose~$m-L$ so that the contraction absorbs these losses. The independence of the fields and the equality of laws~\eqref{e.same.law.shift} then let the argument repeat at the next scale with the same constants.

To estimate the effective diffusivities, we add the fields a block of scales at a time. To first order in the disorder strength, the effect of an added block on the variational problems that determine the effective diffusivity is given by the solution of a Poisson equation forced by the added fields, and the energy of this solution gives the leading decrease~$2\tau^2/d$ per scale of the logarithm of the effective diffusivity. This gives~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic}, and in dimension two planar duality gives the exact formula. The perturbative error need not be smaller than the leading term for every field satisfying~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}, so Proposition~\ref{p.homogenized.coefficient.strict.decay} proves power-law decay by a separate variational argument that does not assume weak disorder: since~$\tau^2>0$, a single field lowers the expected minimal energy with affine boundary values below that of the affine function by a fixed factor smaller than one, and these factors multiply over scales separated by a fixed gap.

\subsubsection{Large-scale regularity and homogenization}

Theorem~\ref{t.C} is proved by comparison with harmonic functions. On a scale where the coefficient is accurately approximated by a constant matrix, as measured through the coarse-grained matrices, a solution is close to a harmonic function, which is close to an affine function on smaller cubes, so the distance of the solution to affine functions decreases from that scale to the next. On the other scales this distance can increase, but only by a bounded factor. The approximation errors on different scales are not independent, since they depend on the same fields. We call a scale \textbf{good} when the fields, and the energies of the variational problems that define the coarse-grained matrices, are small, with a tolerance that grows geometrically with the distance of their scale from the given scale. Independence of the fields then gives a concentration estimate: outside an event of exponentially small probability, only a small proportion of scales fail to be good. The improvement on good scales absorbs the losses on the others, which gives the H\"older exponent in Theorem~\ref{t.C}, and the concentration estimate gives the tail bound~\eqref{e.intro.regularity.minimal.scale}. On cubes larger than the cutoff scale, no further fields enter the coefficient, so averaging over larger cubes only improves the approximation; hence the estimates are uniform in the cutoff.

The large-scale H\"older estimate and the Caccioppoli inequality bound the energy that a solution carries in small cubes. In particular, thin strips around the faces of a cube carry little energy, which we use repeatedly in Section~\ref{sec:mfd-uniqueness}. Theorem~\ref{t.B} does not use the regularity estimates: it follows from the coarse-graining estimates applied to the whole domain.

\subsubsection{Uniqueness of the limit form}

For each~$N$, the rescaled process in~\eqref{e.intro.roughly} is reversible with respect to a measure~$\mu_N$ whose density is proportional to the rescaled coefficient, and its Dirichlet form is~$\int A_N|\nabla u|^2\,dx$ on~$L^2(\mu_N)$, where~$A_N$ is the density of~$\mu_N$ divided by~$\ahom_N$. Apart from a factor that comes from the fields with scales larger than one and does not depend on~$N$, the coefficient~$A_N$ has the law of~$\ahom_N^{-1}\a_N(3^N\,\cdot)$, as in the definition of~$A_{L,M}$ with~$L=M=N$; we therefore call~$N$ the cutoff. The energy determines the elliptic operator, and the reversible measure determines the time parametrization of the paths. The measures~$\mu_N$ converge almost surely, by the construction of multiplicative chaos due to~\citet{Kahane}. Apart from the same factor, the masses that~$\mu_N$ gives to a fixed cube form a nonnegative martingale in~$N$, because the field added at each step multiplies the density by an independent factor with mean one. At weak disorder, a moment of order larger than one of these masses is bounded uniformly in~$N$, so no mass is lost in the limit. The problem is therefore to identify the limit of the energies.

We first prove compactness. Resampling a field whose scale is much smaller than a cube changes the minimal energy on that cube very little. To see this, we partition the cube into smaller boxes and set the field to zero on thin strips around their faces. The parts of the boxes outside the strips are then farther apart than the range of dependence of the field, so the modified energy depends on the field only through its restrictions to these parts, which are independent. The Efron--Stein inequality bounds the variance of a function of independent random variables by half the sum of the expected squared changes caused by resampling them one at a time. Here it bounds the fluctuations by the energy in the boxes, and the regularity estimates make this bound and the energy in the strips decay as a power of that scale. Fields much coarser than the cube are nearly constant on it and act as multipliers. Hence a minimal energy is close, uniformly in the cutoff, to its conditional expectation given finitely many fields, and these conditional expectations form a compact family in~$L^p(\P)$ (Section~\ref{mfd:sec-resampling}). The subsequential limits of the Dirichlet Green operators, which map a source~$f$ to the solution of~$-\nabla\cdot(A_N\nabla u)=f$ in a cube with zero boundary values, define Dirichlet forms, which are measurable with respect to the fields.

Let~$E$ and~$F$ be two such limits. On a small cube, we replace each field coarser than the cube by its value at the center of the cube; this multiplies the restrictions of both forms to the cube by the same random factor. Rescaling the cube, of side~$3^{-k}$, to unit size then turns the energy at cutoff~$N$ into an energy at cutoff~$N-k$, multiplied by this random factor, by~$3^{-(d-2)k}$ and by the deterministic factor~$\ahom_{N-k}/\ahom_N$. The limits of these deterministic factors along the two subsequences that define~$E$ and~$F$ may differ, with a ratio that depends on~$k$. Suppose that this ratio were small on a positive proportion of scales. On cubes of those scales, we could then replace a solution for~$E$ by~$F$-harmonic functions, that is, by minimizers of the~$F$-energy with the same boundary values. This would bound the~$F$-energy of the solution by a small multiple of its~$E$-energy, which by duality contradicts the bounds, uniform in the cutoff, on the positive and negative moments of the energy of the solution with a fixed source. Interchanging~$E$ and~$F$ excludes large ratios. On the remaining scales the ratio is bounded above and below, and the same replacement argument gives the two-sided comparison and equality of the domains (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-C0}). This adapts the normalization argument of~\citet*[Section~3.4]{DGComparison}.

Consider the optimal constants in the two-sided comparison of~$E$ and~$F$: the largest lower constant and the smallest upper constant. The energy measure of a function distributes its energy over space; at a finite cutoff it is~$A_N|\nabla u|^2\,dx$. Resampling one of the fields multiplies the energy measures of both forms by the same weight, and so leaves these constants unchanged; hence they are deterministic. Suppose that they differ. On a small cube with affine boundary values, the minimal~$F$-energy is close to a deterministic multiple of the minimal~$E$-energy; the multiple lies between the two constants and may depend on the scale. On a positive proportion of scales, the multiple lies on the same side of their average, say below it. On a cube of such a scale, with affine boundary values, the minimal~$F$-energy then falls below the upper constant times the minimal~$E$-energy by at least a fixed fraction of the difference of the constants times the minimal~$E$-energy. For any boundary values, the minimal~$F$-energy lies between the lower and the upper constant times the minimal~$E$-energy. Hence the amount by which the minimal~$F$-energy falls below the upper constant times the minimal~$E$-energy is a nonnegative quadratic form in the boundary values that is at most the difference of the constants times the minimal~$E$-energy. Therefore, for the boundary values of a solution that is close to affine on the cube, this amount is close to its value for affine boundary values, up to an error proportional to the difference of the constants. Replacing a solution~$u$ for~$E$ by~$F$-harmonic functions on such cubes then lowers the~$F$-energy below the upper constant times~$E(u)$ by a fixed fraction of the difference of the constants times~$E(u)$, and lower semicontinuity bounds~$F(u)$ by~$E(u)$ times the upper constant lowered by a fixed multiple of the difference of the constants. This contradicts the optimality of the upper constant. If instead the multiple lies above the average on a positive proportion of scales, we interchange the forms and improve the inverse of the lower constant (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-prop}).

Two difficulties remain. First, that amount is proportional to the difference of the optimal constants, so every error must be a small multiple of this difference, not merely small as~$\gmcdelta\to0$. Separate concentration estimates for the two energies do not give this. We instead apply the Efron--Stein bound of Section~\ref{mfd:sec-resampling} to the difference between the minimal~$F$-energy and a multiple of the minimal~$E$-energy. The~$E$-energy of the difference of the two minimizers is at most the square of the difference of the optimal constants, divided by the square of the lower constant, times the minimal~$E$-energy (Lemma~\ref{mfd:lem-diff}), so the resampled quantity fluctuates by a small multiple of the difference of the constants (Proposition~\ref{mfd:prop-conc}). Second, the cubes used for the replacements must carry a fixed fraction of the energy of~$u$. This energy depends on the same fields as the estimates of Lemma~\ref{mfd:in-deterministic}, namely H\"older regularity, energy bounds for H\"older boundary values and harmonic approximation, so it might concentrate on the cubes where these estimates fail. On a single event that does not depend on~$u$, we bound the number of cubes on which these estimates fail along every chain of nested cubes, and we integrate this bound against the energy measure of~$u$.

It remains to show that the proportionality factor is one. Without the factor that comes from the fields with scales larger than one, the coefficients~$A_N$ are stationary and have homogenized matrix equal to the identity, and their coarse-grained matrices on large cubes approach the identity uniformly in~$N$ (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-eta}). Both limits inherit this normalization (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-c1}).

The comparison through optimal constants follows the proof by~\citet*[Theorem~1.9]{GM} that weak subcritical Liouville quantum gravity metrics are unique up to a deterministic factor. These are random metrics on the plane that formally weight Euclidean length by the exponential of a multiple of the Gaussian free field; they are characterized by a list of axioms, to which the word ``weak'' refers. There, a shortest path plays the role of our solution, its length plays the role of the energy, and replacing a segment of the path by a shorter one corresponds to replacing our solution on a cube by the minimizer of the other form with the same boundary values. They controlled the local events encountered along a shortest path by means of the confluence of geodesics, the property that geodesics from a common point to different targets share an initial segment~\citep[Section~4]{GM}. For critical and supercritical metrics,~\citet*[Section~4.2]{DGUniqueness} avoided confluence by modifying the field in annuli visited by a shortest path. Our estimate along chains of nested cubes takes the place of these arguments. It holds on an event that does not depend on the solution, so it can be integrated against the solution's energy measure, and it works in every dimension~$d\geq2$. The uniqueness results for metrics characterize the metrics satisfying a list of axioms. We instead prove the properties that the argument needs for the limits of our energies: consistency under restriction to subcubes, locality, and the rule that multiplying the coefficients~$A_N$ by a positive continuous function multiplies the energy measure of the limit form by the same function.

\subsubsection{Construction of the limiting diffusion}

We pair the unique limit form with the limit~$\mu$ of the reversible measures. The densities of~$\mu_N$ have no bound uniform in the cutoff, but the mass that~$\mu_N$ gives to a ball is at most a random constant times a power of its radius, with exponent arbitrarily close to, and below,~$d$, uniformly in~$N$. This bound controls integrals against~$\mu_N$ by the energy of the integrand, which lets us pass to the limit in the variational problems for the resolvents of the processes killed on leaving a cube. Smoothing the measure-valued sources of these problems and applying the regularity estimates gives convergence of the killed resolvents, uniformly in the starting point.

Tightness comes from a bound on the probability of leaving a small cube in a short time, which we prove from two ingredients. The first is a bound on the Laplace transform of the exit time, obtained through a resolvent problem on the cube. The second is a local maximum principle, a bound on the supremum of a subsolution by its average over a larger cube, which we obtain by Moser iteration (iterated energy estimates for powers of the subsolution) with respect to the reversible measure (Section~\ref{s.tightness}). The same bound shows that, with high probability, the paths stay in a large cube over bounded time intervals. We can therefore recover the whole-space resolvents from the killed ones. Iterating these resolvents determines the joint laws at finitely many times, and tightness gives convergence in probability on continuous path space, locally uniformly in the starting point.

To upgrade to almost-sure convergence, we compare the energies at two finite cutoffs, adapting an argument of~\citet{DGComparison} for random metrics in the form strengthened by~\citet*[Lemma~3.11]{Devlin2024}. Suppose that, on a positive proportion of scales, the ratio of the deterministic factors that multiply the energies at the two cutoffs were smaller than one by a fixed amount. Harmonic extensions on disjoint cubes of these scales would then bound one energy by a fixed fraction, smaller than one, of its limit, which is positive; this contradicts the convergence in probability. Excluding such ratios, and interchanging the cutoffs, gives comparisons with summable error probabilities, and the Borel--Cantelli lemma gives almost-sure convergence of the energies. Almost-sure bounds uniform in the cutoff then transfer this convergence to the killed resolvents and the path laws.

The remaining properties in Theorem~\ref{t.A} have two sources: those of the paths come from power-law decay of the effective diffusivities, and those of the invariant measure and the transition laws come from singularity of~$\mu$. Power-law decay between arbitrary scales makes the limiting paths leave small cubes faster than Brownian paths. To prove that~$\mu$ is singular, we weight the joint law of the environment and a spatial point by the stationary part of~$\mu$. This exponentially tilts the laws of the fields at the point, and the strong law of large numbers shows that the approximating densities diverge at almost every point with respect to~$\mu$, whereas they vanish at Lebesgue-almost every point. The transition laws are absolutely continuous with respect to the invariant measure, so its singularity and the bounds on its mass in small balls exclude Gaussian transition laws.

\subsection*{Use of artificial intelligence}

The authors developed all the main ideas of this paper in 2024. Versions of GPT were used to check the arguments, and Claude was used to help with the writing. The authors take responsibility for the mathematical content and presentation of the paper.

\section{Coarse-graining theory}\label{s.coarse.graining}

In this section we recall a deterministic coarse-graining theory for uniformly elliptic coefficient fields~$\a:\Rd\to\Rsymp$.  The two coarse-grained matrices of a bounded domain give two-sided bounds on the energy of solutions in that domain, and their difference measures how far the equation is from being homogenized. The main argument of this paper tracks this difference from one scale to the next.

\smallskip

Throughout this section, and in contrast to those that follow, no probabilistic structure is used: the coefficient field is arbitrary and fixed.  We define the matrices and record their subadditivity under partitions of a cube, show that solutions satisfy Poincar\'e and Caccioppoli inequalities with constants given by the coarse-grained matrices, estimate how the matrices change under a perturbation of the coefficient field, and combine these into the general coarse-graining estimate used later.  The definitions and estimates are from~\citet{AK.HC} and~\citet{ASD}.

\subsection{Notation}
\label{s.notation}

For a matrix~$A$, the symbol~$|A|$ denotes its operator norm.  We identify a positive scalar coefficient with the corresponding scalar multiple of~$\Id$. For the coarse-grained matrices~$\a(U)$ and~$\a_*(U)$ defined in~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrices}, superscripts denote matrix powers; for example,~$\a_*^{-1}(U)=(\a_*(U))^{-1}$. A coefficient field used to construct a coarse-grained matrix is written after a semicolon, as in~$\a(U;\a^{-1})$.

For a permutation~$\pi$ of~$\{1,\ldots,d\}$,~$n\in\Z$, and~$z\in\Rd$, define
\begin{equation}
\label{e.triadic.simplex.def}
\spx_n^\pi(z)  \coloneqq  z + 3^n  \Bigl\{ (x_1,\ldots,x_d) \in\Rd \,:\, -\frac12 < x_{\pi(1)} < x_{\pi(2)} < \cdots < x_{\pi(d)} < \frac12  \Bigr\}
\,.
\end{equation}
We call~$\spx_n^\pi(z)$ a \textbf{Kuhn simplex} of the cube~$z+\cu_n$; these~$d!$ simplices form its \textbf{Kuhn triangulation}.

For~$n\leq m$, each Kuhn simplex of a cube of side~$3^m$ is partitioned, up to null sets, by the Kuhn simplices of the cubes of side~$3^n$ that it contains. Moreover, the cube~$z+\cu_n$ is the union of the simplices~$\spx_n^\pi(z)$ over all permutations~$\pi$, up to null sets, and~$|\spx_n^\pi(z)|=3^{nd}/d!$.

\subsubsection*{Function spaces}
For~$s\in (0,1)$ and~$p\in [1,\infty)$, the fractional Sobolev seminorm on the cube~$\cu_m$ is defined by
\begin{equation}
\label{e.fractional.Sobolev.seminorm}
[  u ]_{\underline{W}^{s,p}(\cu_m)}
 \coloneqq
\biggl(
s
\fint_{\cu_m} \int_{\cu_m}
\frac{|u(x) - u(y)|^p}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}
\, dx \, dy
\biggr)^{\! \nf1p}
\,.
\end{equation}
At~$s=1$, the seminorm is the classical first-order one,~$\|\nabla u\|_{\underline L^p(\cu_m)}$. For a bounded Lipschitz set~$U$, the same formulas define~$[\,\cdot\,]_{\underline W^{s,p}(U)}$. We set
\begin{equation}
\label{e.positive.Wsp.norm.convention}
\|u\|_{\underline W^{s,p}(U)}
\coloneqq
[u]_{\underline W^{s,p}(U)}+
|U|^{-\nf{s}{d}}\|u\|_{\underline L^p(U)}\,.
\end{equation}
For~$s\in(0,1]$,~$q\in(1,\infty)$,~$N\in\N$, and~$F:U\to\R^N$, define the inhomogeneous volume-normalized negative Sobolev norm by
\begin{equation}
\label{e.negative.Wsp.norm.convention}
\|F\|_{\Wminusul{-s}{q}(U)}
\coloneqq
\sup_{\substack{\varphi\in C^\infty(\overline U;\R^N)\\ \varphi\neq0}}
\frac{\left|\fint_U F\cdot\varphi\right|}
{[\varphi]_{\underline W^{s,q'}(U)}+
|U|^{-\nf{s}{d}}\|\varphi\|_{\underline L^{q'}(U)}}\,.
\end{equation}
When~$q=2$ and~$N=d$, we write
\begin{equation}
\label{e.hatted.negative.norm.convention}
\|F\|_{\Hminusuls{-s}(U)}
\coloneqq
\sup_{\substack{\varphi\in C^\infty(\overline U;\Rd)\\ \varphi\neq0}}
\frac{\left|\fint_U F\cdot\varphi\right|}
{[\varphi]_{\underline H^s(U)}+
|U|^{-\nf{s}{d}}\|\varphi\|_{\underline L^2(U)}}\,.
\end{equation}
Here and below,~$\underline H^s=\underline W^{s,2}$.  If~$F\in(H^s(U;\Rd))^*$, then the numerator in~\eqref{e.hatted.negative.norm.convention} is interpreted as~$|U|^{-1}|\langle F,\varphi\rangle|$; by density of smooth functions, the supremum is then the corresponding dual norm. For every nonempty finite set~$A$, write
\begin{equation}
\label{e.average.sum.convention}
\avsum_{z\in A}c_z
\coloneqq
\frac1{\#A}\sum_{z\in A}c_z\,.
\end{equation}
In particular, if a cube~$U$ is the disjoint union of equal-volume cubes~$(Q_z)_{z\in A}$, then
\begin{equation}
\label{e.average.normalized.norm.partition}
\avsum_{z\in A}
\|f\|_{\underline L^p(Q_z)}^p
=
\|f\|_{\underline L^p(U)}^p\,.
\end{equation}
We work with the (volume-normalized) Besov-type seminorm defined in the cube~$\cu_m$ for exponents~$s \in (0,1)$ and~$p,q,r\in [1,\infty)$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{e.Besov.Morrey.seminorm}
[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{p,q,r}(\cu_m)}
 \coloneqq
\biggl(
s
\sum_{n=-\infty}^m
3^{-nsq}
\biggl(
\avsum_{z\in 3^{n-1} \Zd \cap \cu_m, \, z+\cu_n \subseteq \cu_m}
\bigl\| u - (u)_{z+\cu_n} \bigr\|_{\underline{L}^p(z+\cu_n)}^r
\biggr)^{\!\nf qr} \biggr)^{\!\nf1q} \,.
\end{equation}
For~$r=p$, it reads
\begin{equation}
\label{e.Besov.seminorm}
[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{p,q,p}(\cu_m)}
 =
\biggl(
s \sum_{n=-\infty}^m
3^{-nsq}
\biggl(
\avsum_{z\in 3^{n-1} \Zd \cap \cu_m, \, z+\cu_n \subseteq \cu_m}
\bigl\| u - (u)_{z+\cu_n} \bigr\|_{\underline{L}^p(z+\cu_n)}^p
\biggr)^{\!\nf qp} \biggr)^{\!\nf1q} \,.
\end{equation}
The corresponding norm is~$\|f\|_{\underline B^s_{p,q,r}(\cu_m)} \coloneqq[f]_{\underline B^s_{p,q,r}(\cu_m)} +3^{-sm}|(f)_{\cu_m}|$. By Lemma~\ref{l.Wsp.vs.Bspp}, there exists~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, for every~$s\in(0,1)$ and~$p\in[1,\infty)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.Wsp.vs.Bspp.intro}
C^{-1} [  u ]_{\underline{W}^{s,p}(\cu_m)}
\leq
[ u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{p,p,p}(\cu_m)}
\leq
C [  u ]_{\underline{W}^{s,p}(\cu_m)}
\,.
\end{equation}
The corresponding norms are equivalent for each fixed~$s,p$; unlike the seminorm comparison, their constants need not be uniform in these parameters.

\smallskip

We allow each of~$p,q,r$ to equal~$\infty$.  If~$p=\infty$, the volume-normalized~$L^p$ norm is replaced by the~$L^\infty$ norm; if~$r=\infty$, the average over cube centers is replaced by a supremum; and if~$q=\infty$, the sum over scales is replaced by a supremum.  We also allow~$s=1$ when~$q=\infty$.

\smallskip

When~$q=r=\infty$, the Besov seminorm is equivalent to the H\"older seminorm of order~$s$ for every~$p$. More precisely,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.Besov.to.Holder}
\begin{aligned}
C^{-1}s \sup_{x,y\in \cu_m, \, x\neq y}
\frac{|u(x) - u(y)| }{|x-y|^s }
&\leq
[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{1,\infty,\infty}(\cu_m)}
\leq
[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{\infty,\infty,\infty}(\cu_m)} \\
&\leq
C \sup_{x,y\in \cu_m, \, x\neq y}
\frac{|u(x) - u(y)| }{|x-y|^s }\,.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first inequality of~\eqref{e.Besov.to.Holder} is the only nontrivial one, and it is the Campanato characterization of H\"older spaces.

\smallskip

For~$s\in(0,1]$ and~$q,r\in[1,\infty]$, define
\begin{equation}
\label{e.Besov.weaknorm.def}
\| f \|_{\Besov{-s}{r}{q}(\cu_m)}
 \coloneqq
\biggl(
s\sum_{n=-\infty}^m
3^{nsq}
\biggl(
\avsum_{z\in 3^{n} \Zd \cap \cu_m}
\bigl| (f)_{z+\cu_n} \bigr|^r
\biggr)^{\!\nf qr} \biggr)^{\!\nf1q} \,.
\end{equation}
If~$q^{-1}+(q')^{-1}=r^{-1}+(r')^{-1}=1$ and either~$s<1$ or~$(s,q)=(1,1)$, then Lemma~\ref{l.circ.norm.dominates} gives, for every~$f,g$ for which the right side is finite and~$fg\in L^1(\cu_m)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.duality.splitting}
\biggl| \fint_{\cu_m} f g \biggr|
\leq
C s^{-1}  \| g \|_{\underline{B}^{s}_{1,q',r'}(\cu_m)}
\| f \|_{\Besov{-s}{r}{q}(\cu_m)}\,.
\end{equation}
For a bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$, set
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		& L^2_{\pot}(U)\coloneqq\nabla H^1(U),
		\qquad
		\Lpoto(U)\coloneqq\nabla H^1_0(U),\\
		& L^2_{\sol}(U)\coloneqq\Lpoto(U)^\perp,
		\qquad
		\Lsolo(U)\coloneqq L^2_{\pot}(U)^\perp.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
		\label{e.sols.and.pots}
\end{equation}
All orthogonal complements in~\eqref{e.sols.and.pots} are taken in~$L^2(U;\Rd)$.

\subsection{Coarse-grained matrices}
\label{ss.cg.mats}

We denote the linear space of solutions~$u$ of~$-\nabla \cdot \a \nabla u = 0$ in an open set~$U \subseteq \Rd$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{e.local.solutions.general}
\mathcal{A}(U; \a)  \coloneqq  \{ u \in H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(U) : -\nabla \cdot \a \nabla u = 0 \quad \text{in $U$}\}
\,.
\end{equation}
For every~$p, q \in \Rd$ and bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$, we define the quantity
\begin{equation} \label{e.J.general}
J(U,p,q; \a)  \coloneqq  \sup_{v \in \mathcal{A}(U;\a)\cap H^1(U)} \fint_{U} \Bigl( -\frac{1}{2} \nabla v \cdot \a \nabla v - p \cdot \a \nabla v + q \cdot \nabla v \Bigr)
\,.
\end{equation}
The supremum is attained in~$H^1(U)$, and the maximizer is unique up to an additive constant. We denote its mean-zero representative by~$v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a)$, so that
\begin{equation*}
	(v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a))_U=0\,.
\end{equation*}
For a bounded convex domain~$U \subseteq \Rd$, define the~\textbf{coarse-grained matrices}~$\a(U)$ and~$\a_*(U)$ by the identity, valid for every~$p,q\in\Rd$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.coarse.grained.matrices}
J(U, p,q; \a) \eqqcolon \frac{1}{2} p \cdot \a(U) p + \frac{1}{2} q \cdot \a_{*}^{-1}(U) q - p \cdot q \,.
\end{equation}
These matrices can also be defined variationally~\citep[Lemma~10.5]{AKMBook}: for~$p, q \in \Rd$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.variational.a}
\frac12 p \cdot \a(U) p  = \min_{u \in p\cdot x + H_0^1(U)} \fint_{U}\frac12 \nabla u \cdot \a \nabla u
=  \max_{\g \in \Lsol(U)} \fint_U \Bigl(-\frac12 \g \cdot \a^{-1} \g + p \cdot \g  \Bigr) \, ,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{e.variational.astar}
\frac12 q \cdot \a^{-1}_*(U) q
=
\max_{w \in H^1(U)} \fint_{U} \Bigl(-\frac12 \nabla w \cdot \a \nabla w + q \cdot \nabla w \Bigr)
= \min_{\g \in q + \Lsolo(U)} \fint_{U} \frac12 \g \cdot \a^{-1} \g \, .
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Properties of the coarse-grained matrices}

We use the following identities and inequalities; see~\citet{AK.Book,AK.HC}.  Throughout,~$U\subseteq\Rd$ is a bounded convex domain.

\smallskip

The coarse-grained matrices are bounded by averages of the field, in the Loewner order:
\begin{equation} \label{e.ord.bounds.truncated}
\Bigl( \fint_{U} \a^{-1} \Bigr)^{\!-1} \leq \a_{*}(U) \leq \a(U)
\leq
\fint_{U} \a  \, .
\end{equation}

\smallskip

The coarse-grained matrices are~\textbf{subadditive}: for every partition~$\{ U_i \}_{i=1}^N$ of~$U$ (up to a Lebesgue null set)
\begin{equation}
\label{e.subadditivity.a}
\a(U) \leq  \sum_{i=1}^N \frac{|U_i|}{|U|} \a(U_i)  \qand \a_*(U) \geq \biggl( \sum_{i=1}^N \frac{|U_i|}{|U|}\a^{-1}_*(U_i) \biggr)^{-1} \, ,
\end{equation}
which generalizes the first and third inequalities of~\eqref{e.ord.bounds.truncated}. Equivalently, for every~$p, q \in \Rd$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.subadditivity.J}
J(U, p, q; \a) \leq
\sum_{i=1}^N \frac{|U_i|}{|U|} J(U_i, p , q; \a) \,.
\end{equation}
The spatial averages of the gradient and the flux of the maximizer can be written explicitly in terms of the coarse-grained matrices: for~$p, q \in \Rd$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.v.spatial.averages}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
& \fint_U
\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q; \a)
=
- p + \a_{*}^{-1}(U) q
\\ &
\fint_U
\a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q; \a)
=
q - \a(U) p\,.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation}
We also denote, for~$e \in \Rd$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.v.oneslot.def}
v(\cdot,U,e;\a)  \coloneqq  v\bigl(\cdot,U,0, \a_{*} (U) e  \bigr)
\,,
\end{equation}
so that by~\eqref{e.v.spatial.averages} we have
\begin{equation} \label{e.solution.avg.grad.flux.identity}
\begin{pmatrix}
\bigl(\nabla v(\cdot,U,e; \a) \bigr)_{U}  \\
\bigl( \a   \nabla v(\cdot,U,e; \a) \bigr)_{U}
\end{pmatrix}
=
\begin{pmatrix}
e  \\  \a_{*}(U)  e
\end{pmatrix}
\,.
\end{equation}
The identity~\eqref{e.J.plug.identity} below gives
\begin{equation}
\label{e.J.gap}
J(U,e,\a_{*}(U) e\,;\a)
=
\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\a(U)-\a_{*}(U)\bigr)e\,.
\end{equation}
For every~$w\in\A(U;\a)\cap H^1(U)$ and~$p,q\in\Rd$, the first variation of~\eqref{e.J.general} gives
\begin{align}
\label{e.firstvar}
q\cdot \fint_U \nabla w - p \cdot \fint_U \a \nabla w
=
\fint_U \nabla w \cdot \a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q; \a)\, .
\end{align}
The second variation gives
\begin{align}
\label{e.secondvar}
& J(U,p,q; \a) - \fint_U \Bigl  ( -\frac12 \nabla w \cdot \a \nabla w -p\cdot \a \nabla w+ q\cdot \nabla w   \Bigr  )
\notag \\ & \qquad \qquad\qquad
=
\fint_U \frac12 \bigl ( \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) - \nabla w \bigr )\cdot \a \bigl ( \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) - \nabla w \bigr )\,.
\end{align}
It follows that~$J$ can be written as the energy of the maximizer: for every~$p,q\in\Rd$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.Jenergy.v}
J(U,p,q;\a) = \fint_U \frac12 \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) \cdot \a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a)
\,.
\end{equation}
The Euler equation also gives
\begin{equation}
\label{e.J.lin}
J(U,p,q;\a) = \frac12 \Bigl( q\cdot \fint_U \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) - p \cdot \fint_U \a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) \Bigr) \, .
\end{equation}
The coarse-grained matrix~$\a_{*}(U)$ bounds the energy of an arbitrary~$H^1(U)$ function from below in terms of the spatial average of its gradient: for every~$u\in H^1(U)$,
\begin{align}
\label{e.energymaps}
\frac12 \bigl(  \nabla u \bigr)_U \cdot \a_{*}(U) \bigl(  \nabla u \bigr)_U
\leq
\fint_U \frac12 \nabla u \cdot \a \nabla u
\,.
\end{align}
The dual inequality is, for every~$\h\in L^2_{\sol}(U)$,
\begin{align}
\label{e.energymaps.flux}
\frac12\bigl( \h \bigr)_U \cdot \a^{-1} (U) \bigl( \h \bigr)_U
\leq
\fint_U \frac12 \h \cdot \a^{-1} \h
\,.
\end{align}
For every constant positive-definite matrix~$\b\in\Rsymp$ and every~$u\in\mathcal A(U;\a)\cap H^1(U)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.fluxmaps}
\bigl| \bigl(  ( \a - \b) \nabla u \bigr)_U
\bigr|
\leq
2\biggl(\max_{|e|=1}J(U,e,\b e\,;\a)\biggr)^{\nf12}
\biggl( \fint_U \frac12 \nabla u \cdot \a \nabla u  \biggr)^{\!\nf12}
\, ,
\end{equation}
in particular, with the coarse-graining error~$\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,1}$ of Definition~\ref{d.mathcal.E} below, for every~$m \in \Z$ and every~$u\in\mathcal A(\cu_m;\a)\cap H^1(\cu_m)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.CG.elliptic.operator}
3^{-s m} \| ( \a-\b) \nabla u \|_{\Besov{-s}{2}{1}(\cu_m)}
\leq
4s^{-1}
|\b|^{\nf12}
\|\a^{\nf12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_m)}
\mathcal{E}_ {s,\infty,1} (\cu_m;\a,\b)
\,.
\end{equation}
The bound~\eqref{e.fluxmaps} follows from~\eqref{e.firstvar},~\eqref{e.Jenergy.v}, and the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality.

The identity~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrices} can be rewritten for every symmetric matrix~$\b \in \R^{d \times d}_{\sym}$ as
\begin{align}
\label{e.J.plug.identity}
J(U,e,\b e; \a)
&=
\frac12 e \cdot \bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) e
+
\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\b - \a_{*}(U)\bigr) \a_{*}^{-1}(U) \bigl(\b - \a_{*}(U)\bigr) e
\,.
\end{align}
If~$\b\in\Rsymp$, then completing the square in the first argument of~$J$ instead of the second gives
\begin{align}
\label{e.J.plug.identity.inverses}
J(U,\b^{-1} e, e; \a)
&
=
\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\a_{*}^{-1}(U) - \a^{-1}(U)\bigr)e
+
\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\b^{-1} - \a^{-1} (U)\bigr) \a(U) \bigl(\b^{-1} - \a^{-1} (U)\bigr) e
\,.
\end{align}
Applying~\eqref{e.J.plug.identity} and~\eqref{e.J.plug.identity.inverses} with~$\b^{-\nf12} e$ and~$\b^{\nf12} e$, respectively, in place of~$e$ yields
\begin{align}
\label{e.J.half.half.identity}
J(U,\b^{-\nf12}e,\b^{\nf12} e;\a)
&
=
\frac12 e \cdot \b^{-\nf12}\bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{-\nf12}e
\notag \\ &  \quad
+
\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\Id - \b^{-\nf12}\a_{*}(U)\b^{-\nf12}\bigr) \b^{\nf12}\a_{*}^{-1}(U) \b^{\nf12} \bigl(\Id - \b^{-\nf12}\a_{*}(U)\b^{-\nf12}\bigr) e
 \\ &
 \label{e.J.half.half.identity.numbertwo}
=
\frac12 e \cdot \b^{\nf12}\bigl( \a_{*}^{-1}(U)- \a^{-1}(U)  \bigr) \b^{\nf12}e
\notag \\ &  \quad
+
\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\Id - \b^{\nf12}\a^{-1}(U)\b^{\nf12}\bigr) \b^{-\nf12}\a(U) \b^{-\nf12} \bigl(\Id - \b^{\nf12}\a^{-1}(U)\b^{\nf12}\bigr) e
\,.
\end{align}

\begin{lemma}
\label{l.sharp.compare.J}
For every bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$ and~$\b\in\Rsymp$,
\begin{align}
\label{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin}
\max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)
&
\leq
\frac12 \bigl|\b^{-\nf12} \bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{-\nf12}  \bigr|
+
\frac12 \bigl| \b^{-\nf12} \a^{\nf12}_*(U) -  \b^{\nf12} \a^{-\nf12}_*(U)\bigr|^2
\notag \\ &
\leq
2 \max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)  \,,
\end{align}
and
\begin{align}
\label{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin2}
\max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)
&
\leq
\frac12 \bigl|\b^{\nf12}   \bigl( \a^{-1}(U) - \a^{-1}_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{\nf12}  \bigr|
+
\frac12 \bigl| \b^{\nf12} \a^{-\nf12}(U) -  \b^{-\nf12} \a^{\nf12}(U)\bigr|^2
\notag \\ &
\leq
2 \max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)  \, .
\end{align}
In particular, there exists a universal constant~$C<\infty$ such that
\begin{multline}
\label{e.coarse.grained.matrix.deviation.by.J}
\bigl|\b^{-\nf12} \bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{-\nf12} \bigr|
+
\bigl|\b^{\nf12} \a^{-1}_*(U) \b^{\nf12} - \Id \bigr|^2
+
\bigl|\b^{-\nf12} \a(U) \b^{-\nf12} - \Id\bigr|^2
\\
\leq
C\max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a) \bigl(1 + \max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a) \bigr)  \, .
\end{multline}

\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For positive-semidefinite matrices~$M$ and~$N$,
\begin{equation*}
\max\{|M|,|N|\}
\leq |M+N|
\leq |M|+|N|
\leq2|M+N|\,.
\end{equation*}
Applying this inequality to the two summands in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity} proves~\eqref{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin}; applying it to the two summands in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity.numbertwo} proves~\eqref{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin2}.

For every positive-definite matrix~$A$ and~$J\geq0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.from.a.ratios.to.J}
\frac 12 \bigl| (\Id-A)A^{-1} (\Id-A) \bigr|
\leq J
\quad \implies \quad
\bigl|\Id-A\bigr|^2 \leq 4J(1+J)\,.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the hypothesis implies
\begin{equation*}
\bigl| \Id - A \bigr|^2
\leq
2J|A|
\leq
2J|\Id - A | + 2J
\leq
\frac12 |\Id - A |^2 + 2J^2 + 2J\,.
\end{equation*}
Since
\begin{equation*}
(\Id-A)A^{-1}(\Id-A)
=
(\Id-A^{-1})A(\Id-A^{-1})\,,
\end{equation*}
apply~\eqref{e.from.a.ratios.to.J} with~$A=(\b^{-\nf12}\a_*(U)\b^{-\nf12})^{-1}$ in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity} and with~$A=(\b^{\nf12}\a^{-1}(U)\b^{\nf12})^{-1}$ in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity.numbertwo}. This gives
\begin{align}
\label{e.aratios.to.J}
\lefteqn{
\max\Bigl\{
\bigl|\b^{-\nf12}\a(U)\b^{-\nf12} - \Id \bigr|^2
\, ,\,
\bigl|\b^{\nf12}\a_{*}^{-1}(U; \a)\b^{\nf12} - \Id \bigr|^2
\Bigr\}
} \qquad &
\notag \\ &
\leq
10\sup_{|e|=1}
J(U,\b^{-\nf12}e,\b^{\nf12} e; \a)
\bigl( 1 + \sup_{|e|=1}
J(U,\b^{-\nf12}e,\b^{\nf12} e; \a)  \bigr)
\,.
\end{align}
Combining~\eqref{e.aratios.to.J} with~\eqref{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin} gives~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrix.deviation.by.J}.
\end{proof}

For every~$\lambda>0$, bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$ and~$p,q\in\Rd$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.homogeneous.J}
J(U,p,q\,;\a) = J(U,\lambda^{-\nf12} p, \lambda^{\nf12}q \,; \lambda \a) \,.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Coarse-grained elliptic and functional inequalities}
\label{ss.cg.ellipticity}

Following~\citet[(2.39)]{AK.HC}, we attach to each cube~$\cu_m$ the coarse-grained ellipticity constants~$\lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)$ and~$\Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)$. The resulting Poincar\'e and Caccioppoli inequalities depend on these constants rather than on the pointwise ellipticity ratio.

\smallskip

The weights~$(1-3^{-s})3^{-ns}$ sum to one over~$n\geq0$. For~$0<s\leq2$, their normalizing factor satisfies~$\nf49s\leq1-3^{-s}\leq(\log3)s$.

\begin{definition}[Coarse-grained ellipticity constants]
For~$s>0$,~$q\in[1,\infty)$,~$m\in\Z$, and a uniformly elliptic field~$\a:\cu_m\to\Rsymp$, define the \textbf{coarse-grained ellipticity constants} by
\begin{equation}
\label{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
& {\Lambda}_{s,q}(\cu_m\,;\a)
\coloneqq
\biggl(
(1-3^{-sq}) \sum_{k=-\infty}^{m}
3^{-sq(m-k)}
\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\bigl| \a(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|^{\nf q2}
\biggr)^{\!\nf{2}{q}}
\,, \\  &
{\lambda}_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)
\coloneqq
\biggl((1-3^{-sq}) \sum_{k=-\infty}^{m}
3^{-sq(m-k)}
\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\bigl| \a_{*}^{-1}(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|^{\nf q2}
\biggr)^{\!- \nf{2}{q}}
\,,
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation}
and, for~$q=\infty$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity.infty}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
& {\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\cu_m\,;\a)
\coloneqq
\sup_{k\in(-\infty,m]\cap \Z}
3^{-2s(m-k)}
\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\bigl| \a(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
\,, \\  &
{\lambda}_{s,\infty}(\cu_m;\a)
\coloneqq
\biggl(
\sup_{k\in(-\infty,m]\cap \Z}
3^{-2s(m-k)}
\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\bigl| \a_{*}^{-1}(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
\biggr)^{\!-1}
\,.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation}
When the exponent~$q$ is omitted, it is understood that~$q=1$, that is,
\begin{equation*}
	\Lambda_{s} (\cu_m;\a)
	=
	\Lambda_{s,1} (\cu_m;\a)
	\qqand
	\lambda_{s} (\cu_m;\a)
	=
	\lambda_{s,1} (\cu_m;\a)
	\,.
\end{equation*}
Equation~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity.infty} is the limit of~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity} as~$q\to\infty$.  On~$y+\cu_m$, every subcube~$z+\cu_k$ in these definitions is replaced by~$y+z+\cu_k$, with~$z\in3^k\Zd\cap\cu_m$.
\end{definition}
For every~$m\in\Z$,~$q\in [1,\infty]$ and~$t,s\in (0,\infty)$ with~$t<s$, we have
\begin{equation}
\lambda_{t,q}(\cu_m; \a)
\leq
\lambda_{s,q} (\cu_m; \a)
\leq |\a_*^{-1}(\cu_m;\a) |^{-1}
\leq
|\a(\cu_m;\a)|
\leq
\Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m; \a)
\leq
\Lambda_{t,q}(\cu_m; \a)
\,.
\label{e.ellipticities.monotone.ordered}
\end{equation}
There exists~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, for every~$s\in(0,1]$ and~$p,q\in[1,\infty]$ with~$p\leq q$,
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		& \Lambda_{s,q} (\cu_m;\a)
		\leq
		C s^{\nf2q - \nf2p}
		\Lambda_{s,p} (\cu_m;\a)
		\leq
		C s^{\nf2q - 2}
		\Lambda_{s} (\cu_m;\a)
		\,,
		\\
		& \lambda^{-1}_{s,q} (\cu_m;\a)
		\leq
		C s^{\nf2q - \nf2p}
		\lambda^{-1}_{s,p} (\cu_m;\a)
		\leq
		C s^{\nf2q -2}
		\lambda^{-1}_{s} (\cu_m;\a)
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\label{e.ellipticities.change.q}
\end{equation}
For every~$k\in(-\infty,m]\cap\Z$, applying~\eqref{e.ellipticities.monotone.ordered} on the subcubes and comparing the definitions gives
\begin{equation}
	\label{e.bound.one.cube.by.lambdas}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		&
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
		\leq
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\Lambda_{s,q} (z+\cu_k; \a)
		\leq 3^{2s(m-k)} \Lambda_{s,q} (\cu_m;\a)
		\,,
		\\ &
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a_{*}^{-1}(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
		\leq
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\lambda_{s,q}^{-1} (z+\cu_k; \a)
		\leq 3^{2s(m-k)} \lambda_{s,q}^{-1} (\cu_m;\a)
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
\end{equation}

We measure the error in replacing~$\a$ by a constant matrix~$\a_0$ through the quantities~$J(z+\cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf12}e,\a_0^{\nf12}e;\a)$ on the subcubes~$z+\cu_l$.

\begin{definition}[Coarse-graining error]
\label{d.mathcal.E}
For~$s\in(0,1]$,~$p,q\in[1,\infty]$,~$m,n\in\Z$ with~$n\leq m$, a uniformly elliptic field~$\a:\cu_m\to\Rsymp$, and a matrix~$\a_0\in\Rsymp$, define the \textbf{coarse-graining error} by
\begin{align*}
\lefteqn{
\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m , n; \a, \a_0 )
} \ \notag \\ &
=
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&
\biggl(
(1-3^{-sq})
\sum_{l=-\infty}^n
3^{-sq(n-l)}
\biggl(
\avsum_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf p2}
\biggr)^{\!\nf qp}
\biggr)^{\!\nf1q}
\,,
\quad  p,q<\infty\,,
\\ &
\biggl(
(1-3^{-sq})
\sum_{l=-\infty}^n
3^{-sq(n-l)} \!\!
\max_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf q2}
\biggr)^{\!\nf1q}
\,,
\quad p=\infty,\ q<\infty\,,
\\
&
\sup_{l \in (-\infty,n]\cap \Z}
3^{-s(n-l)}
\biggl(
\avsum_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf p2}
\biggr)^{\!\nf 1p}
\,,
\quad p<\infty,\ q=\infty \,,
\\ &
\sup_{l \in (-\infty,n]\cap \Z}
3^{-s(n-l)}
\max_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf 12}
\,,
\quad p = q = \infty \,.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{align*}
On~$y+\cu_m$, every subcube~$z+\cu_l$ in this definition is replaced by~$y+z+\cu_l$, with~$z\in3^l\Zd\cap\cu_m$.  We use the abbreviation
\begin{equation*}
\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
\coloneqq
\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m , m; \a, \a_0 )
\,.
\end{equation*}
When the exponent~$q$ is omitted, it is understood that~$q=1$.
\end{definition}

The definition gives, for~$m\in\Z$,~$p\in[2,\infty]$,~$q\in[1,\infty]$, and~$0<t<s\leq1$,
\begin{equation}
\max_{|e| =1}  J\bigl(\cu_m,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr)^{\nf12}
\leq
\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
\leq
\mathcal{E}_{t,p,q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
\,,
\label{e.mathcalE.monotone.ordered}
\end{equation}
and, for every~$k\in (-\infty,m]\cap \Z$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.bound.one.cube.by.mathcalE}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
&
\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q} (z+\cu_k;\a, \a_0)
\leq 3^{s(m-k)} \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q}  (\cu_m;\a,\a_0)
\,,
\\ &
\avsum_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (z+\cu_k;\a, \a_0)
\leq 3^{(s+d(\nf1p-\nf1q)_+)(m-k)}
\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q}  (\cu_m;\a,\a_0)
\,.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation}

For a positive scalar~$\a_0$,~$s\in(0,1]$ and~$q\in[1,\infty]$,
\begin{align}
\label{e.bound.Lambdas.by.Es}
\frac12 \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q}
(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)^2
&
\leq
\max\bigl\{
|\a_0|^{-1} \Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)
\,,
|\a_0| \lambda_{s,q}^{-1} (\cu_m;\a)
\bigr\}
\notag \\ &
\leq
\bigl(1+\sqrt2\,\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q}(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)\bigr)^2
\,.
\end{align}
To see the upper bound, put $J_U=\max_{|e|=1}J(U,\a_0^{-\nf12}e,\a_0^{\nf12}e;\a)$. The ordering $\a_*(U)\leq\a(U)$ and~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrices} imply that each eigenvalue~$t$ of $\a_0^{-1}\a(U)$ or $\a_0\a_*^{-1}(U)$ satisfies $t+t^{-1}-2\leq2J_U$. Thus
\begin{equation*}
\sqrt t\leq1+\sqrt{2J_U}
\quad\text{and}\quad t\leq1+2J_U+\sqrt{2J_U}\,.
\end{equation*}
The first inequality, followed by the weighted $\ell^q$ triangle inequality, gives the upper bound in~\eqref{e.bound.Lambdas.by.Es}. For the lower bound, use $\sqrt{J_U}\leq2^{-\nf12} (|\a_0^{-1}\a(U)|^{\nf12}+|\a_0\a_*^{-1}(U)|^{\nf12})$ and the same triangle inequality. The second eigenvalue bound also gives
\begin{equation*}
\max\{\a_0^{-1}\Lambda_{s,2}(\cu_m;\a),\a_0\lambda_{s,2}^{-1}(\cu_m;\a)\}
\leq1+2\mathcal E_{s,\infty,2}^2(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)+2\mathcal E_{s,\infty,2}(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)\,.
\end{equation*}
Moreover, in the case~$q = 2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.bound.Lambdas.by.Es.q2}
1+\frac12 \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,2}
(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)^2
\leq
\max\bigl\{
|\a_0|^{-1} \Lambda_{s,2}(\cu_m;\a)
\,,
|\a_0| \lambda_{s,2}^{-1} (\cu_m;\a)
\bigr\} \, .
\end{equation}
By Definition~\ref{d.mathcal.E}, for every~$p,q \in [1,\infty]$ and~$s \in (\nf{d}{p},1)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{e.mathcalE.infty.to.q}
\mathcal{E}_{s, \infty, q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
\leq
\Bigl(
\frac{(1-3^{-sq})}{(1-3^{-q(s-\nf{d}{p})})}
\Bigr)^{\nf1q}
\mathcal{E}_{s - \nf{d}{p}, p, q}(\cu_m;\a,\a_0) \, .
\end{equation}
For~$1\leq q\leq p<\infty$ and~$s>0$,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_{s, \infty, q}(\cu_m; \a, \a_0)
\leq
\mathcal{E}_{\frac{s q}{p}, \infty, p}(\cu_m; \a, \a_0)
\, .
\label{e.compareEqs}
\end{equation}
The same definitions give
\begin{equation}
\Lambda_{s, q}(\cu_m; \a)
\leq
\Lambda_{\frac{s q}{p}, p}(\cu_m; \a)
\qand
\lambda_{s, q}^{-1}(\cu_m; \a)
\leq
\lambda_{\frac{s q}{p}, p}^{-1}(\cu_m; \a) \, .
\label{e.compareLambdaqs}
\end{equation}
By H\"older's inequality, there exists a universal constant~$C<\infty$ such that, for~$0<s<t\leq1$,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_{t,\infty,1}(\cu_m , n; \a, \a_0)
\leq
\frac{Ct}{(t-s)^{\nf12}\, s^{\nf12}} \, \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,2}(\cu_m , n; \a, \a_0)
\,.
\label{e.mathcalE.one.vs.mathcalE.two}
\end{equation}

The coarse-grained ellipticity constants satisfy the following stability estimate, which is~\citet[Lemma~2.5]{ASD}.

\begin{lemma}[Stability of the coarse-grained ellipticity constants]
\label{l.lambdas.stability}
There exists a constant~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, for every~$q\in[1,\infty]$,~$s,t\in(0,\nf12]$ with~$s<t$, and~$x\in\cu_0$ such that~$x+\cu_{-1}\subseteq\cu_0$, we have the estimates
\begin{equation}
\label{e.lambda.stability}
\lambda_{t,q}^{-1}(x+\cu_{-1}\,;\a)
\leq
\frac{C}{1-2s} \Bigl( \frac{t}{t-s}\Bigr)^{\! \nf 2q} \lambda_{s,q}^{-1}(\cu_{0}\,;\a) \,,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\label{e.big.Lambda.stability}
\Lambda_{t,q}(x+\cu_{-1}\,;\a)
\leq
\frac{C}{1-2s} \Bigl( \frac{t}{t-s}\Bigr)^{\! \nf 2q} \Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_{0}\,;\a)\,,
\end{equation}
and, for every~$\a_0\in\Rsymp$ (see~\citet[(2.62)]{ASD}),
\begin{equation}
\label{e.mathcalE.stability}
\mathcal{E}_{t,\infty,2}(x+\cu_{-1}\,;\a,\a_0)
\leq
\frac{C}{(1-2s)^{\nf12}} \Bigl( \frac{t}{t-s}\Bigr)^{\! \nf 12}
\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,2}(\cu_0\,;\a,\a_0)
\,.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

The next proposition is taken from~\citet[Lemma~2.3]{AK.HC}.
\begin{proposition}[Coarse-grained Poincar\'e inequality]
\label{p.coarse.grained.poincare}
For every~$s\in (0,1]$, $q \in [1,\infty]$, $m \in \Z$, every~$u\in H^1(\cu_m)$ and~$\h \in L^2_{\mathrm{sol}}(\cu_m)$,
\begin{equation}
3^{-s m} [ \nabla u ]_{\Besov{-s}{2}{q}(\cu_m)}
\leq
(1-3^{-sq})^{-\nf1q}
\lambda_{s,q}^{-\nf12}  (\cu_m; \a) \| \a^{\nf 12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_m)}
\,,
\label{e.besov.grad.poincare}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
3^{-s m} [ \h ]_{\Besov{-s}{2}{q}(\cu_m)}
\leq
(1-3^{-sq})^{-\nf1q}
\Lambda^{\nf12 }_{s,q} (\cu_m; \a)  \| \a^{-\nf 12} \h \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_m)}
\, .
\label{e.besov.flux.poincare}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

We combine the functional inequality
\begin{equation}
[ u ]_{\underline{B}^{1-s}_{2,\infty}(\cu_0)}
\leq
C [ \nabla u ]_{\Besov{-s}{2}{1}(\cu_0)}
\,,
\label{e.nabla.u.detach}
\end{equation}
with~\eqref{e.besov.grad.poincare} and the bound~$\| u - (u)_{\cu_0} \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)} \leq C \liminf_{s\uparrow 1}  [ u ]_{\underline{B}^{1-s}_{2,\infty}(\cu_0)}$, and let~$s\uparrow1$. This gives, for some~$C(d)<\infty$ and every~$u\in H^1(\cu_0)$,
\begin{equation}
\| u - (u)_{\cu_0} \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)}
\leq
C
\lambda_{1,1}^{-\nf12}  (\cu_0; \a) \| \a^{\nf 12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)}
\,.
\label{e.CG.Poincare.mean.zero}
\end{equation}
For zero boundary data we also have the following estimate. Let~$w\in H^1_0(\cu_0)$ be the \textbf{torsion function} of~$\cu_0$, that is, the solution of
\begin{equation*}
\left\{
\begin{aligned}
& -\Delta w = 1 & \text{in} & \ \cu_0\,, \\
& w = 0 & \text{on} & \ \partial \cu_0\,.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\end{equation*}
Then, for every~$u\in H^1_0(\cu_0)$,
\begin{equation*}
